<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WA616BHJibW9EY2YSGZ2EE`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}
5	A growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training data analysis. Membership inference approaches estimate whether a model has seen specific items during training: Min-K\% \citep{shi2024mink} and Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang2024minkpp} use token-level log-likelihood statistics, while Time Travel \citep{golchin2024timetravel} probes models with temporal cues and \citet{oren2024proving} prove contamination through exchangeability tests. Performance-based detectors identify contamination through statistical anomalies \citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique,deng2024investigating}. \citet{balloccu2024leak} provide a broader taxonomy of leakage detection strategies. These methods share a common validation practice: they verify detection accuracy against web-overlap or n-gram labels \citep{li2024contamination} that classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \emph{differential item functioning} (whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts). This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our ground truth analysis (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). Work on LLM memorization \citep{carlini2022quantifying, tirumala2022memorization} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. \citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.
6	
7	\paragraph{IRT in LLM Evaluation.}
8	IRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated: \citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT to NLP, \citet{vania2021irt} assessed test set discriminability, and \citet{rodriguez2021evaluation} demonstrated that not all evaluation examples are equally informative. Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \citep{hardy2026scalability}, ATLAS \citep{li2025atlas} for computerized adaptive testing of LLMs, and competency-focused IRT evaluation in medical domains \citep{luo2025medirt}. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \citep{habba2026growingpains}, which uses IRT calibration to establish anchor items for cross-temporal score equating, providing a prescriptive framework for maintaining comparability via fixed-parameter calibration \citep[FPC;][]{kim2019fpc}. Our work supplies the diagnostic foundation: identifying which items exhibit differential functioning and quantifying the magnitude of temporal instability. Their anchor-item approach can directly consume our stable-item set as pre-screened equating anchors~\citep{kolen2014equating}. Our finding that 31\% of items exhibit temporal DIF directly informs anchor selection by identifying which items should \emph{not} serve as anchors. To our knowledge, no prior work applies DIF to benchmark temporal comparability---contamination detection and psychometric evaluation optimization have developed as separate threads.
9	
10	\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}
11	The Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\chi^2$ test \citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \citep{raju1988area}; \citet{zumbo1999handbook} and \citet{penfield2007dif} provide comprehensive comparisons. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance, millsap2011invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the MH procedure; our contribution is the application context and the empirical findings it reveals. The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\theta$-conditioning (as sensitivity check), and a random-split control (\Cref{sec:temporal}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}
14	Several concurrent approaches address benchmark reliability from complementary angles. The Benchmark Health Index~\citep{zhu2026bhi} and Benchmark$^2$~\citep{qian2026benchmark2} perform meta-evaluation of static benchmark quality at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses \emph{when} measurement properties shift. MMLU-CF~\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items; DIF monitoring provides a lower-cost alternative when reconstruction is infeasible, identifying which existing items remain measurement-invariant rather than replacing them. Combined with the prescriptive equating framework of Growing Pains~\citep{habba2026growingpains} (discussed above), these approaches span the full lifecycle from diagnosis (our work) to reconstruction~\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} to calibration~\citep{habba2026growingpains}. \citet{maeda2025difllm} predict which item features associate with DIF in educational settings; we instead detect DIF empirically across LLM cohorts at scale. Dynamic benchmarks \citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Broader surveys \citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and item-level opacity as systemic challenges that our DIF analysis directly addresses.
15

Our finding that 31\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF directly informs anchor selection by identifying which items should \\emph{not} serve as anchors. To our knowledge, no prior work applies DIF to benchmark temporal comparability---contamination detection and psychometric evaluation optimization have developed as separate threads.\n\n\\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{zumbo1999handbook} and \\citet{penfield2007dif} provide comprehensive comparisons. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance, millsap2011invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the MH procedure; our contribution is the application context and the empirical findings it reveals. The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning (as sensitivity check), and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}\nSeveral concurrent approaches address benchmark reliability from complementary angles. The Benchmark Health Index~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} and Benchmark$^2$~\\citep{qian2026benchmark2} perform meta-evaluation of static benchmark quality at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses \\emph{when} measurement properties shift. MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items; DIF monitoring provides a lower-cost alternative when reconstruction is infeasible, identifying which existing items remain measurement-invariant rather than replacing them. Combined with the prescriptive equating framework of Growing Pains~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} (discussed above), these approaches span the full lifecycle from diagnosis (our work) to reconstruction~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} to calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}. \\citet{maeda2025difllm} predict which item features associate with DIF in educational settings; we instead detect DIF empirically across LLM cohorts at scale. Dynamic benchmarks \\citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Broader surveys \\citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and item-level opacity as systemic challenges that our DIF analysis directly addresses.\n",
      "numLines": 15,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 15
    }
  },

    "newString": "\\citet{millsap1993dif}, \\citet{zumbo1999handbook}, and \\citet{penfield2007dif} provide comprehensive reviews",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}\nA growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training data analysis. Membership inference approaches estimate whether a model has seen specific items during training: Min-K\\% \\citep{shi2024mink} and Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang2024minkpp} use token-level log-likelihood statistics, while Time Travel \\citep{golchin2024timetravel} probes models with temporal cues and \\citet{oren2024proving} prove contamination through exchangeability tests. Performance-based detectors identify contamination through statistical anomalies \\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique,deng2024investigating}. \\citet{balloccu2024leak} provide a broader taxonomy of leakage detection strategies. These methods share a common validation practice: they verify detection accuracy against web-overlap or n-gram labels \\citep{li2024contamination} that classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \\emph{differential item functioning} (whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts). This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our ground truth analysis (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). Work on LLM memorization \\citep{carlini2022quantifying, tirumala2022memorization} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. \\citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT in LLM Evaluation.}\nIRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated: \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT to NLP, \\citet{vania2021irt} assessed test set discriminability, and \\citet{rodriguez2021evaluation} demonstrated that not all evaluation examples are equally informative. Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \\citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \\citep{hardy2026scalability}, ATLAS \\citep{li2025atlas} for computerized adaptive testing of LLMs, and competency-focused IRT evaluation in medical domains \\citep{luo2025medirt}. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \\citep{habba2026growingpains}, which uses IRT calibration to establish anchor items for cross-temporal score equating, providing a prescriptive framework for maintaining comparability via fixed-parameter calibration \\citep[FPC;][]{kim2019fpc}. Our work supplies the diagnostic foundation: identifying which items exhibit differential functioning and quantifying the magnitude of temporal instability. Their anchor-item approach can directly consume our stable-item set as pre-screened equating anchors~\\citep{kolen2014equating}. Our finding that 31\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF directly informs anchor selection by identifying which items should \\emph{not} serve as anchors. To our knowledge, no prior work applies DIF to benchmark temporal comparability---contamination detection and psychometric evaluation optimization have developed as separate threads.\n\n\\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{zumbo1999handbook} and \\citet{penfield2007dif} provide comprehensive comparisons. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance, millsap2011invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the MH procedure; our contribution is the application context and the empirical findings it reveals. The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning (as sensitivity check), and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}\nSeveral concurrent approaches address benchmark reliability from complementary angles. The Benchmark Health Index~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} and Benchmark$^2$~\\citep{qian2026benchmark2} perform meta-evaluation of static benchmark quality at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses \\emph{when} measurement properties shift. MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items; DIF monitoring provides a lower-cost alternative when reconstruction is infeasible, identifying which existing items remain measurement-invariant rather than replacing them. Combined with the prescriptive equating framework of Growing Pains~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} (discussed above), these approaches span the full lifecycle from diagnosis (our work) to reconstruction~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} to calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}. \\citet{maeda2025difllm} predict which item features associate with DIF in educational settings; we instead detect DIF empirically across LLM cohorts at scale. Dynamic benchmarks \\citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Broader surveys \\citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and item-level opacity as systemic challenges that our DIF analysis directly addresses.\n",

Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \\citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \\citep{hardy2026scalability}, IRT-based evaluation quality~\\citep{kraus2024irt}, ATLAS \\citep{li2025atlas} for computerized adaptive testing of LLMs, and competency-focused IRT evaluation in medical domains \\citep{luo2025medirt}. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \\citep{habba2026growingpains}, which uses IRT calibration to establish anchor items for cross-temporal score equating, providing a prescriptive framework for maintaining comparability via fixed-parameter calibration \\citep[FPC;][]{kim2019fpc}. Our work supplies the diagnostic foundation: identifying which items exhibit differential functioning and quantifying the magnitude of temporal instability. Their anchor-item approach can directly consume our stable-item set as pre-screened equating anchors~\\citep{kolen2014equating}. Our finding that 31\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF directly informs anchor selection by identifying which items should \\emph{not} serve as anchors. To our knowledge, no prior work applies DIF to benchmark temporal comparability---contamination detection and psychometric evaluation optimization have developed as separate threads.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}",
          " The Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{millsap1993dif}, \\citet{zumbo1999handbook}, and \\citet{penfield2007dif} provide comprehensive reviews. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance, millsap2011invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the MH procedure; our contribution is the application context and the empirical findings it reveals. The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning (as sensitivity check), and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal})."

    "newString": "Dynamic benchmarks \\citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} and self-evolving update frameworks~\\citep{ying2024automating,xu2025benchmark} mitigate contamination by construction",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}\nA growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training data analysis. Membership inference approaches estimate whether a model has seen specific items during training: Min-K\\% \\citep{shi2024mink} and Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang2024minkpp} use token-level log-likelihood statistics, while Time Travel \\citep{golchin2024timetravel} probes models with temporal cues and \\citet{oren2024proving} prove contamination through exchangeability tests. Performance-based detectors identify contamination through statistical anomalies \\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique,deng2024investigating}. \\citet{balloccu2024leak} provide a broader taxonomy of leakage detection strategies. These methods share a common validation practice: they verify detection accuracy against web-overlap or n-gram labels \\citep{li2024contamination} that classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \\emph{differential item functioning} (whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts). This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our ground truth analysis (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). Work on LLM memorization \\citep{carlini2022quantifying, tirumala2022memorization} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. \\citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT in LLM Evaluation.}\nIRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated: \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT to NLP, \\citet{vania2021irt} assessed test set discriminability, and \\citet{rodriguez2021evaluation} demonstrated that not all evaluation examples are equally informative. Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \\citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \\citep{hardy2026scalability}, IRT-based evaluation quality~\\citep{kraus2024irt}, ATLAS \\citep{li2025atlas} for computerized adaptive testing of LLMs, and competency-focused IRT evaluation in medical domains \\citep{luo2025medirt}. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \\citep{habba2026growingpains}, which uses IRT calibration to establish anchor items for cross-temporal score equating, providing a prescriptive framework for maintaining comparability via fixed-parameter calibration \\citep[FPC;][]{kim2019fpc}. Our work supplies the diagnostic foundation: identifying which items exhibit differential functioning and quantifying the magnitude of temporal instability. Their anchor-item approach can directly consume our stable-item set as pre-screened equating anchors~\\citep{kolen2014equating}. Our finding that 31\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF directly informs anchor selection by identifying which items should \\emph{not} serve as anchors. To our knowledge, no prior work applies DIF to benchmark temporal comparability---contamination detection and psychometric evaluation optimization have developed as separate threads.\n\n\\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{millsap1993dif}, \\citet{zumbo1999handbook}, and \\citet{penfield2007dif} provide comprehensive reviews. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance, millsap2011invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the MH procedure; our contribution is the application context and the empirical findings it reveals. The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning (as sensitivity check), and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}\nSeveral concurrent approaches address benchmark reliability from complementary angles. The Benchmark Health Index~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} and Benchmark$^2$~\\citep{qian2026benchmark2} perform meta-evaluation of static benchmark quality at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses \\emph{when} measurement properties shift. MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items; DIF monitoring provides a lower-cost alternative when reconstruction is infeasible, identifying which existing items remain measurement-invariant rather than replacing them. Combined with the prescriptive equating framework of Growing Pains~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} (discussed above), these approaches span the full lifecycle from diagnosis (our work) to reconstruction~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} to calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains}. \\citet{maeda2025difllm} predict which item features associate with DIF in educational settings; we instead detect DIF empirically across LLM cohorts at scale. Dynamic benchmarks \\citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Broader surveys \\citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and item-level opacity as systemic challenges that our DIF analysis directly addresses.\n",

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	We adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.
5	
6	\subsection{Data}
7	\label{sec:data}
8	
9	The primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.
10	
11	\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}
12	\label{sec:mhdif}
13	
14	In educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.
15	
16	The Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\citep{holland1988mh} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \times 2$ contingency table:
17	
18	\begin{equation}
19	\label{eq:mh}
20	\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}} = \frac{\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \,/\, T_k}{\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \,/\, T_k}\,,
```

[Director 指令] [Patch — K=10 非单调性 + framing 优化] Reviewer 审查 K sensitivity 给了 1 个 warning：

**K=10 的 35.9% 是非单调 peak**：K=5→7→10→20 对应 31.3%→32.7%→35.9%→32.9%。K=10 偏高可能因更细 strata 捕获 ability-DIF interactions；K=20 回落因每 stratum ~94 models MH 统计量不稳定。

**要求**：§4.2 的 K sensitivity 句子改为包含非单调性说明。Reviewer 建议的 framing 很好——"K=5 is the most conservative choice"：

建议改为类似：
"C\% is stable across stratification choices ($K \geq 5$: 31.3\%--35.9\%; $K{=}5$ yields the most conservative estimate). The non-monotonic pattern ($K{=}10$ peak at 35.9\%) reflects finer strata capturing residual ability--DIF interactions, while $K{=}20$ shows instability from small strata (${\sim}94$ models each); $K{=}3$ inflates to 39.4\% due to under-stratification."

同时 Reviewer 确认 DEFF reclassification 结论的整合方式正确。如果已经填了 PLACEHOLDER 数据，也请确认 §3.2 的 269 items (6.9%) 数据已写入。

请 commit 所有待提交改动。

```text
12	\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\% Measurement Instability}
13	\label{sec:temporal}
14	
15	\looseness=-1
16	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; 95\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\%, 38.1\%]; family-level cluster bootstrap CI $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$). Three independent non-independence corrections converge on approximately 27--29\%: simple-mean DEFF correction (29.2\%), graduated family de-duplication at $K{=}20$ (29.1\%; \Cref{tab:lofo}), and family-level cluster bootstrap lower bound (26.7\%). The weighted-mean DEFF variant yields 13.5\%, but its effective cluster size ($\bar{m}{=}254$) implies each family member is functionally identical---unrealistic for models with distinct training data; with only ${\sim}$30 families, weighted-mean ICC estimates are unstable~\citep{valliant2013practical}. All reclassifications are significance-driven ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ unchanged); the $[13.5\%, 29.2\%]$ range reflects power uncertainty, not disagreement about DIF magnitude. We adopt ${\approx}$27--29\% as our primary corrected estimate, with 13.5\% as a conservative lower bound (\Cref{app:deff,app:uncertainty}). Even the most conservative bound exceeds the random-split baseline by $75\times$. Leave-one-family-out analysis confirms no single family drives the signal ($|\Delta\mathrm{C\%}| \leq 2.93$\,pp). C\% is stable across strata ($K \geq 5$: max $|\Delta\mathrm{C\%}| = 4.60$\,pp).
17	
18	\looseness=-1
19	Three controls confirm genuine temporal change (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield C\% of 0.18\% (2023) and 1.3\% (2024), both ${\geq}23\times$ below temporal C\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$, confirming at most ${\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).
20	
21	\looseness=-1
```

20	\looseness=-1
21	Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), so monitoring cannot be replaced by pre-screening. Circularity analysis confirms DIF removal is the \emph{least} disruptive non-random strategy ($z = -9.5$ vs.\ accuracy-gap $z = -157$; \Cref{app:fig_downstream}), motivating equating over removal~\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}. We recommend annual MH-DIF audits whenever $\geq$500 models accumulate per cohort, with three action levels grounded in $\rho$-degradation curves (\Cref{app:fig_rho_degradation}): \emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$), \emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\%$: dual rankings), \emph{Act} ($\geq 25\%$: stable-item rankings as primary; DIF pre-screening for equating anchors). Subject-level monitoring is essential (mean $\rho = 0.950$; 22/57 subjects below 0.95).
22

Three non-exclusive mechanisms may drive the observed ${\\approx}$27--29\\% rate: (1)~differential contamination (asymmetric training data exposure); (2)~capability evolution (genuine skill acquisition); (3)~training methodology shifts (RLHF, post-training). The ETS baseline of 5--15\\% C\\%~\\citep{clauser1998dif} is for concurrent demographic groups sharing the same test administration; the comparison to temporal LLM cohorts is illustrative, not normative.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nOur experimental evidence constrains this ambiguity through four exclusion arguments. \\emph{Ability confounds} explain at most 2.4~pp: 1:1 matching yields C\\% $= 28.9\\%$ vs.\\ unmatched 31.3\\% (Cohen's $d = -0.17$; \\Cref{sec:temporal}). \\emph{Domain-level improvement} explains ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); DIF is predominantly item-specific, not a byproduct of aggregate progress. \\emph{Item quality defects} are ruled out: expert-annotated errors~\\citep{gema2024mmlu} show no enrichment among DIF-C items ($0.92\\times$, $p = 0.37$). \\emph{Web-overlap labels}~\\citep{li2024contamination} are near-independent of DIF flags (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \\Cref{sec:groundtruth}), reflecting a category error in ground truth rather than detection failure; MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} complements our approach via item reformulation.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThe difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$; \\Cref{sec:temporal}) provides suggestive evidence for capability evolution: hardest-decile DIF-C items are 88.7\\% focal-favoring (2024 better), easiest-decile 96.2\\% reference-favoring. But I should verify whether the current looseness setting is even having an effect—if the paragraph naturally renders at five lines and the looseness parameter isn't reducing it, then pushing it further won't help. Instead, I could try `\emergencystretch` to give TeX more flexibility in spacing.

Actually, the simplest fix is to just cut the audit recommendation from the end since it's already covered in section 5.2, leaving only the Jaccard independence result and ranking preservation statement.

Though removing the recommendation entirely makes the conclusion feel incomplete. I could keep a shorter version—something like "temporal DIF audits should accompany score reporting" or "motivating temporal MH-DIF audits"—that's brief enough to avoid the overflow while still pointing forward.

两个补充实验完成，数据需要 patch 进论文：

**1. Li web-overlap enrichment 95% CI（patch §4.3 + Table 2）**
- Enrichment = 0.978, bootstrap 95% CI [0.944, 1.017]（CI 包含 1.0）
- Fisher exact: OR=0.948, p=0.25
- φ = -0.012
- 注意：Table 2 当前 enrichment=0.964，新 bootstrap 计算=0.978，两者差异来自 bootstrap resampling。建议在正文 §4.3 加 CI，Table 保持 0.964 不变（table 是点估计，正文报 bootstrap CI）

§4.3 当前文本 "enrichment ratio is 0.964" 附近，加 bootstrap CI：
→ 建议改为类似 "The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (bootstrap 95% CI: [0.94, 1.02]; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape})"

§4.3 当前 "$\phi = 0.012$, OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15], Jaccard $= 0.206$" 保持不变（已有 OR CI）。

**2. Jaccard permutation null（patch §4.3 + §5.1）**
- Observed Jaccard = 0.206
- Permutation null: mean=0.212, SD=0.0049, 95% CI [0.203, 0.222]
- p = 0.88 (one-sided, observed vs null)
- 结论：observed Jaccard 在 null distribution 之内，overlap 完全由 marginal rates 预测

§4.3 当前 "Jaccard $= 0.206$"：
→ 改为 "Jaccard $= 0.206$ (within permutation null $[0.203, 0.222]$; $p = 0.88$)"

§5.1 discussion 当前 "Web-overlap labels...are near-independent of DIF flags (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \Cref{sec:groundtruth})"：
→ 加 permutation context: "(Jaccard $= 0.206$, indistinguishable from permutation null; \Cref{sec:groundtruth})"

这两个数据点把 "near-independent" 从描述性判断升级为统计性结论。这次不用验证引用。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BXy4UHKPoRgfFQckXGEz6a`</summary>

```text
44	\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}
45	\label{sec:groundtruth}
46	
47	\looseness=-1
48	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Association tests confirm near-independence ($\phi = 0.012$, OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15], Jaccard $= 0.206$). This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method. 
49	\begin{figure}[t]
50	\centering
51	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
52	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
53	\label{fig:dose_response}
54	\end{figure}
55	
56	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
57	\label{sec:structural}
58	
```

</details>

11	\looseness=-1
12	Our experimental evidence constrains this ambiguity through four exclusion arguments. \emph{Ability confounds} explain at most 2.4~pp: 1:1 matching yields C\% $= 28.9\%$ vs.\ unmatched 31.3\% (Cohen's $d = -0.17$; \Cref{sec:temporal}). \emph{Domain-level improvement} explains ${<}0.1\%$ of item-level DIF variance (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); DIF is predominantly item-specific, not a byproduct of aggregate progress. \emph{Item quality defects} are ruled out: expert-annotated errors~\citep{gema2024mmlu} show no enrichment among DIF-C items ($0.92\times$, $p = 0.37$). \emph{Web-overlap labels}~\citep{li2024contamination} are near-independent of DIF flags (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \Cref{sec:groundtruth}), reflecting a category error in ground truth rather than detection failure; MMLU-CF~\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} complements our approach via item reformulation.
13	
14	\looseness=-1
15	The difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$; \Cref{sec:temporal}) provides suggestive evidence for capability evolution: hardest-decile DIF-C items are 88.7\% focal-favoring (2024 better), easiest-decile 96.2\% reference-favoring. This pattern is more consistent with skill acquisition than contamination, which would target items regardless of difficulty; however, temporal DIF is necessary but not sufficient for construct-irrelevant change, and observational DIF alone cannot separate contamination from capability evolution~\citep{suk2026rethinking}. The high aggregate $\rho \approx 0.997$ does not undermine DIF's utility: aggregate stability masks subject-level instability (mean per-subject $\rho = 0.950$; 22/57 below 0.95; five below 0.90). DIF monitoring enables targeted replacement~\citep{zhao2025mmlucf,habba2026growingpains}.
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\enlargethispage*{14\\baselineskip}\n\n\\subsection{What Drives Temporal DIF?}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}. Three non-exclusive mechanisms may drive the observed ${\\approx}$27--29\\% rate: (1)~differential contamination (asymmetric training data exposure); (2)~capability evolution (genuine skill acquisition); (3)~training methodology shifts (RLHF, post-training). The ETS baseline of 5--15\\% C\\%~\\citep{clauser1998dif} is for concurrent demographic groups sharing the same test administration; the comparison to temporal LLM cohorts is illustrative, not normative.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nOur experimental evidence constrains this ambiguity through four exclusion arguments. \\emph{Ability confounds} explain at most 2.4~pp: 1:1 matching yields C\\% $= 28.9\\%$ vs.\\ unmatched 31.3\\% (Cohen's $d = -0.17$; \\Cref{sec:temporal}). \\emph{Domain-level improvement} explains ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); DIF is predominantly item-specific, not a byproduct of aggregate progress. \\emph{Item quality defects} are ruled out: expert-annotated errors~\\citep{gema2024mmlu} show no enrichment among DIF-C items ($0.92\\times$, $p = 0.37$). \\emph{Web-overlap labels}~\\citep{li2024contamination} are near-independent of DIF flags (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \\Cref{sec:groundtruth}), reflecting a category error in ground truth rather than detection failure; MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} complements our approach via item reformulation.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThe difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$; \\Cref{sec:temporal}) provides suggestive evidence for capability evolution: hardest-decile DIF-C items are 88.7\\% focal-favoring (2024 better), easiest-decile 96.2\\% reference-favoring. This pattern is more consistent with skill acquisition than contamination, which would target items regardless of difficulty; however, temporal DIF is necessary but not sufficient for construct-irrelevant change, and observational DIF alone cannot separate contamination from capability evolution~\\citep{suk2026rethinking}. The high aggregate $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ does not undermine DIF's utility: aggregate stability masks subject-level instability (mean per-subject $\\rho = 0.950$; 22/57 below 0.95; five below 0.90). DIF monitoring enables targeted replacement~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf,habba2026growingpains}.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), so monitoring cannot be replaced by pre-screening. Circularity analysis confirms DIF removal is the \\emph{least} disruptive non-random strategy ($z = -9.5$ vs.\\ accuracy-gap $z = -157$; \\Cref{app:fig_downstream}), motivating equating~\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}. We recommend MH-DIF audits whenever $\\geq$500 models accumulate, with three action levels (\\Cref{app:fig_rho_degradation}): \\emph{Monitor} (C\\% $< 15\\%$), \\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\\%$: dual rankings), \\emph{Act} ($\\geq 25\\%$: stable-item rankings as primary). Subject-level monitoring is essential (mean $\\rho = 0.950$; 22/57 subjects below 0.95).\n",

          " Temporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}. Three non-exclusive mechanisms may drive the observed ${\\approx}$27--29\\% rate: (1)~differential contamination (asymmetric training data exposure); (2)~capability evolution (genuine skill acquisition); (3)~training methodology shifts (RLHF, post-training). The ETS baseline of 5--15\\% C\\%~\\citep{clauser1998dif} is for concurrent demographic groups sharing the same test administration; the comparison to temporal LLM cohorts is illustrative, not normative.",
          " ",
          " \\looseness=-1",
          "-Our experimental evidence constrains this ambiguity through four exclusion arguments. \\emph{Ability confounds} explain at most 2.4~pp: 1:1 matching yields C\\% $= 28.9\\%$ vs.\\ unmatched 31.3\\% (Cohen's $d = -0.17$; \\Cref{sec:temporal}). \\emph{Domain-level improvement} explains ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$); DIF is predominantly item-specific, not a byproduct of aggregate progress. \\emph{Item quality defects} are ruled out: expert-annotated errors~\\citep{gema2024mmlu} show no enrichment among DIF-C items ($0.92\\times$, $p = 0.37$). \\emph{Web-overlap labels}~\\citep{li2024contamination} are near-independent of DIF flags (Jaccard $= 0.206$; \\Cref{sec:groundtruth}), reflecting a category error in ground truth rather than detection failure; MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} complements our approach via item reformulation.",

          " ",
          " \\looseness=-1",
          "-Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (bootstrap 95\\% CI: $[0.94, 1.02]$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Association tests confirm near-independence ($\\phi = 0.012$, OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15], Jaccard $= 0.206$, within permutation null $[0.203, 0.222]$; $p = 0.88$). This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method. ",
